51. **"Don't add them to the body's reference list yet" (the coordinator's addition 3) against Safety at One Shot in §2 and §6.** Read as the body's list of reference arms (the five references of §4.4), not its bibliography: Safety at One Shot was cited in §2 and §6 earlier in this revision, because D5 = (a) bounds the one-example patch "in text (related work and a limitation)" and the coordinator's addition 2 asked for the citation if verified. It is not a reference arm. If the coordinator meant the bibliography too, delete the two clauses (§2's last sentence and §6's second sentence) and the BibTeX entry `zhang2026oneshot`.
52. **Option B (decision 28) against the body's stage-1 framing.** Option B makes OLMo-1B's stage 1 development and puts the confirmatory comparison at 7–8B; the body still calls OLMo-2-1B the stage-1 model and lists the testing order of revision 5. The coordinator asked only for the 7–8B tier's description; the rest of option B is for the lead and the user before the stage-1 pre-registration. **Revision 6c:** UW-21 (FOCUS fcf79a2) keeps OLMo-2-1B's P1–P6 as the confirmatory primary family under any re-plan option, which matches the body's stage-1 framing.

53. **The user's decisions of 17 Oct against FOCUS.** The brief relays four decisions (Olmo-3-7B-Instruct added; "ambitious default with fallbacks"; the "Compute-Matched" title kept with token matching; option B with C as the fallback). FOCUS at origin head 6368d66 records none of them, and the stage-1 pre-registration DRAFT revision 2 marks items that rely on the "ambitious default" rule [USER-CONFIRM]: "The lead's chat has not confirmed this rule" (its checklist item FC-1). Resolution: applied as relayed and recorded as relayed (decisions 26, 28, 48); the lead records them in FOCUS. **Revision 6c: resolved.** FOCUS fcf79a2 records the user's confirmation of the rule in the lead's chat (07:21 UTC; FC-1 closed) and UW-14 (the title and token matching). Olmo 3 was not among them: the lead then reported it as not yet confirmed (decision 48; the user confirmed it later, FOCUS 8dac6ad, revision 6e). Option B with C as the fallback is still recorded only as relayed; UW-21 keeps OLMo-2-1B's P1–P6 confirmatory under any re-plan option (conflict 52).
54. **The Qwen clause against the overlap rule.** That Qwen2.5 saturates under recipe B is part of the concurrent submission's same-recipe family contrast. The brief asks for one neutral clause with the reason. Resolution: the clause gives the class ("saturated … (above 55 pp)") and no number, keeps the citation for the comparison, and "What this is" lists it as a fifth exception to "nothing in the body is a result". The reason is FOCUS's: the 7 Oct 17:44 UTC collapse of Qwen2.5-7B (4.8% → 87.0%), the frozen bars' "E > 55 is saturated, like Qwen" (19:14 UTC), and the user's "Llama/OLMo only" (20:58 UTC). Not stated in the body: Qwen was dropped before the per-family rule existed (risk 56). **Revision 6c:** the reason now sits in §6 as one sentence (the lead's ruling 2), with the model (Qwen2.5-1.5B), the recipe and the rate but no erosion number and no date; §4.1 points to §6, and "What this is" moves the exception from §4.1 to §6. The body now says that Qwen was dropped before the per-family rule existed.
55. **The HarmBench input (review B W15) against FOCUS.** Review B says the claim rule applies the classifier to answers of up to 2,048 tokens. FOCUS 8 Oct 10:14 UTC records the 512-token clip for the development check, and the stage-1 DRAFT keeps it. The brief asks to mark the length [TO BE FIXED in the stage-1 pre-registration]; §4.2 does so neutrally ("clipped to 512 tokens or in full (up to 2,048)"). If the lead keeps the clip, the sentence can state it as design. **Revision 6c: resolved** by the lead's ruling 1: the clip is design (§4.2; Appendix C.10), as HarmBench's evaluation script does.
56. **Which HarmBench classifier.** FOCUS names `cais/HarmBench-Mistral-7b-val-cls` (the validation classifier); review B cites `cais/HarmBench-Llama-2-13b-cls` as "the official classifier for text behaviors in HarmBench". The body says only "HarmBench's classifier". Name the model in Appendix C or the claim rule before submission, and check which one HarmBench's paper reports (not checked here). **Revision 6c:** the body names the validation classifier (§4.2), and Appendix C.10 gives both ids; the test classifier is a candidate sensitivity check only. HarmBench's evaluation script defaults to the Llama-2-13b classifier; which classifier the HarmBench paper's main tables use was not checked here (CITE-CHECK 36).
57. **Safety at One Shot in §2, §4.3 and §6.** D5 = (a) bounds the post-hoc one-example patch in text (not a reference; no claim covers it). Revision 6b's §4.3 lists its data-only analogue (the example added to the training data) as a candidate arm [TO BE FIXED in the stage-1 pre-registration]. They are different repairs, so §6's sentence ("Post-hoc repairs other than the references, such as the one-example patch (Zhang et al., 2026a), are not compared") still holds if the arm is added.
58. **"Five references" against the planned sixth.** The abstract, §1, §3.2 and §4.4 say five; §4.4 adds a sixth, the lower learning rate, as [PLANNED; to be pre-registered]. Resolution: "five" kept while the sixth is planned; Table 1's caption now says "references". If the pre-registration adds it, update every count, including §1's "dominant only if it beats five references".
59. **The customer and the safety prompt.** Table 1's legend and §4.4 let the customer run the safety prompt, because a test-time prefix is in the customer's own requests. A provider's safety gate (Microsoft, 2026) may evaluate without it, so it may not help the customer pass the gate; the body claims nothing about the gate. Decision 46.
60. **"Controls only the data".** §1 said the owner "often controls only the data" and §6 that a closed-service customer "can change only the data"; revision 6b's §3.1 says the customer can also set the learning rate and epochs (brief item 2; review B W3). Resolution: §1 and §6 now say "the data and a few training settings" and "the data and some hyperparameters"; the abstract's conditional "When only the training data may change" is kept.
61. **"Smaller differences stay inconclusive" (§6) against §3.2's rules.** A difference smaller than δ with a tight interval is labelled *equivalent*, not inconclusive. Resolution: §6's clause is cut (brief item 7).
62. **The brief's item 5 ("references on all six a0 seeds") against §4.4's three seeds.** §4.4 still says three seeds for the safety prompt, adapter scaling and Safe LoRA, and decision 19 defaults to seeds 1101–1103. Resolution: the TO BE FIXED sentence names both options ("three, or all six a0 seeds"); the stated three stay until the pre-registration rules.
63. **Body pages.** The measure is unchanged at 7.96 pages and the heading sits at the same height as in revision 6, because the cuts balanced the additions to within a line; a probe with one more line gave 8.00 pages ("What changed in revision 6b", Length).
64. **The brief's scope against the stage-1 decisions.** The brief for revision 6c applies two of the body's ten [TO BE FIXED] items (token matching; the HarmBench input) and keeps three items for the freeze. FOCUS fcf79a2 also settles the analysis model (UW-02, LW-01), the closed testing family (UW-04), the over-refusal baseline of *worse* (UW-05, rule R1.3), one primary τ_O (UW-06) and the label order (UW-08 (a)). Resolution: followed the brief; the body keeps these markers (decision 49). **Revision 6d: resolved** by the lead's rulings 1–3 (decision 49); decision 53 lists what is still not applied.
65. **Seeds.** Table 1 and Appendix C.3 say random removal at eight seeds and no repair at six; UW-03 (layout D1; addendum §6 item 2) runs $a_0$ and $a_{\mathrm{R}}$ at seeds 1101–1107 (seven each; R_1108 is not run) and the method arms at 1102–1107. Not changed: outside the brief (decision 49). **Revision 6d: resolved** (the lead's ruling 2): Table 1, Appendix C.3 and §4.4 (conflict 70).
66. **The over-refusal bank.** §4.2 says 100 prompts; UW-07a adds 400 harmless PHTest prompts to the primary over-refusal bank if REQ-19's free check shows a six-seed MDE above 5 pp (fallback: the development 100 only). The addition is conditional, so §4.2 stays (decision 49). **Revision 6d: resolved** (the lead's ruling 3): §4.2 states the conditional extension and "never from the held-out bank".
67. **R_tok's details.** The W1 builder extract's construction of R_tok (W1-03: per-unit walks in a sealed order, seeds 1102–1104, one-sided bounds only, never in P1–P6) is marked as its writer's interpretation, and the token axis is the lead's reading (W1-02 item 4). Addendum §6 item 5 says only "a secondary arm at 3 seeds, funded tier", and FOCUS's UW-14 "pre-registered secondary arms in the funded tier … fallback: the analysis only". Resolution: §3.2 and Appendix C.3 state those facts, plus the analysis's report-only status and its dose curve from W1-02 (marked binding) and the pre-registration DRAFT's token-accounting item; no axis, construction or test is stated (decision 51). **Revision 6d:** the axis is stated (the lead's ruling 4: total row tokens, target tokens beside it); R_tok's construction and test are still not stated.
68. **"As HarmBench's own evaluation script does" (the brief).** Checked on the script (CITE-CHECK 36). The body says only "512-token window"; Appendix C.10 says the rest. The script's default classifier is the Llama-2-13b one, not the validation classifier that the study uses; the clip is the shared part.
69. **Which Qwen model.** The lead's ruling names Qwen2.5-1.5B, the main-study candidate that the user dropped on 7 Oct (20:58 UTC). Qwen2.5-7B also saturated (FOCUS 7 Oct 17:44 UTC) and is also out. §4.1 says "Qwen2.5 … is outside this study"; §6 names only the 1.5B model, as the ruling does. The later Qwen runs (FOCUS 9 and 11 Oct) are side work for the concurrent submission, which §6 cites as the only later evidence.
70. **Rulings 2 and 6 at §4.4.** Ruling 2 fixes "any other place that says … $a_0$ at 6"; ruling 6 keeps "the post-hoc reference seeds". §4.4's "(three, or all six $a_0$ seeds)" is both. Resolution: the open choice is kept (three, or all), and the $a_0$ count follows D1: "(three, or all seven $a_0$ seeds)". US-01 gives the same reading ("a0 1101–1106 (1101–1107 under D1)").
71. **The full-bank clause against ruling 3.** §3.2's [TO BE FIXED] sentence asked whether feasibility uses "the full XSTest and OR-Bench-Hard banks"; their remainder is the hidden over-refusal bank, which UW-07b and ruling 3 exclude ("never from the held-out bank"). Resolution: the clause is cut; the sentence keeps only the open non-inferiority question.
72. **The dollar sign in the ruling's sentence.** The ruling writes the check's cost as zero dollars with a dollar sign, which is a math delimiter in this file and in KaTeX, and the body states no money. Resolution: "a free, pre-registered check".
73. **The planning MDE against HB-C and $\Phi$.** Not in the rulings, but the old figure ("after Holm's first step … about 5 pp") belongs to the replaced model. Resolution: the number is dropped and the placeholder kept (decision 52).
74. **"S-a and S-aW reported" (FOCUS LW-01) against "pre-registered sensitivities" (addendum §6 item 1; ruling 1 (a)).** The ruling and the addendum agree; FOCUS's "reported" is read as the same thing. The body says "pre-registered sensitivity analyses".
75. **"No file paths" (ruling 5) against the script's name.** Ruling 5 names `evaluate_completions.py`; Appendix C.10 names that public script by its file name, with no directory, repository path or hash.
76. **The second half of the label-order marker.** Revision 6b's sentence also left open "whether a significant difference below δ gets its own label". UW-08 (a)'s recommended text, which the user took, says the row prints "significant, below the SESOI". Resolution: Appendix C.11 states it, with SESOI written out as "the smallest effect of interest"; the body sentence keeps only ruling 1 (e)'s words.
77. **"So an adverse repair is never reported as inconclusive" (§3.2) against R1.3.** Under R1.3 a repair that is significantly above its comparator on over-refusal but feasible against θ0 is not *worse*, so it can read *inconclusive* or another label. The clause held under the user's 18:31 UTC rule, which R1.3 narrows (UW-05 (b), the user's choice). Resolution: the clause is cut; Appendix C.11 gives R1.3's reason. **Revision 6e: confirmed by the lead (ruling 6);** Appendix C.11 now also says what such a repair's label is (conflict 83).
78. **The flag's name (revision 6e's brief against the sources).** Revision 6e's brief gave the flag another name; LW-09's default (with RC-02), DRAFT §3.7 and §4.5 name it "fails answers end", and "comparator fails answers end" for a failing comparator; FOCUS records LW-09 at its default. Resolution (revision 6e): the sources' names, in Appendix C.11 only; the body said "still read and flagged". **Revision 6f: RESOLVED (the lead's ruling 1):** the name is "fails answers end" everywhere; §4.2 now names it.
79. **UW-15's "harmful-prompt cause" against "benign-looking" (§4.5, Appendix C.5, the Impact Statement).** LW-10 corrects the description, but it is outside the brief. Resolution: not changed; decision 54. **Revision 6f: RESOLVED (the lead's ruling 2; decision 54):** §4.5, Appendix C.5 and the Impact Statement describe the plant as PKU red-team prompts with PKU-safe, non-refusing answers.
80. **B3's "the frozen rule".** Grade 1 is the user's 8 Oct label rule (decided), while the stage-1 pre-registration is not frozen, so "frozen" would mislead in the paper. Resolution: "the rule of §3.2". **Revision 6f: RESOLVED (the lead's ruling 4): confirmed; unchanged.**
81. **Gate A's mean erosion in Appendix C.11 against "nothing in the body is a result".** UW-13's rule needs its constant, 45.11 pp, OLMo-2-1B's Gate A mean $E$, and C1 states δ as about 11% of it. Resolution: the number, C1's wording and the per-seed ratio range (10.4–11.5%) sit in Appendix C.11, as Appendix C.1 already uses 0.75 of Gate A's mean $E$ (33.8 pp); the body's clause gives no number, and the per-seed $E$ values are not in the paper. **Revision 6f: RESOLVED (the lead's ruling 4): confirmed; unchanged** (the 45.11 pp makes δ_fam reproducible).
82. **Decision 2 and the fill-in "A repair is worse" against R1.3.** Both said that every significantly adverse contrast gives *worse*. Resolution: both note R1.3's narrowing (conflict 77).
83. **Ruling 6's "still feasible at τ_O*" against the three feasibility states.** Under LW-06 an arm can also be *undetermined*, and R1.3 needs *infeasible*. Resolution: Appendix C.11 says "not infeasible at $\tau_O^*$ (feasible or undetermined)", which follows from R1.3 and LW-06 as written, and adds the same for task loss (R1.3's second half). **Revision 6f: RESOLVED (the lead's ruling 4):** confirmed: only an arm that is infeasible at $\tau_O^*$ (its interval's lower end above the limit) and significantly above its comparator is *worse (O)*. Appendix C.11 says exactly this and is unchanged.
84. **"Olmo 3 the first model cut" (§6) against the cut order.** FOCUS 8dac6ad cuts 70B, then 32B, then Olmo 3 7B. §6's clause ("at 7–8B it is planned on both families and Olmo-3-7B-Instruct, funding-gated, with one family as the fallback and Olmo 3 the first model cut") sits in a sentence about 7–8B, and §4.1's marker says "first 7–8B model cut", which the coordinator keeps. Resolution: unchanged; if a reader takes §6's phrase as the first cut overall, add "7–8B" there (one word). **Revision 6f: RESOLVED (the lead's ruling 4): confirmed; unchanged.**
85. **"Correct it wherever the notes give only USD 8–10" (the brief).** Decision 48 already gave both figures; it now labels USD 8–10 as Gate A rung 1 only (two seeds) and USD 16–44 as the three-seed Gate A. No other place in the notes gave an Olmo 3 cost.
86. **UW-15's wording against "each training-signal diagnostic".** The user took UW-15 (FOCUS fcf79a2): headline item (2) reads "does each diagnostic find a concentrated, harmful-prompt cause". LW-10 F2, at its default, counts only A1 and the Self-Inf-N-style score toward "finds a known cause". The lead's ruling 3 inserts "training-signal" in the abstract, §1, contribution 2 and §6. Resolution: applied as ruled. It narrows the user's adopted text by one word, so the lead may report it to the user: the weekend instruction (FOCUS 00b99a4) covers pending choices, and UW-15 was decided. If the user wants UW-15's exact words, remove "training-signal" in those four places and keep §4.5's counting sentence and contribution 2's construction-check sentence.
87. **The reason for the construction checks.** The brief says that the text-only scorers "can read the plant's text"; DRAFT §8.5 F2 says that they "read only row text and θ0, so their planted-cell ranks are fixed when the plant is drawn". Resolution: Appendix C.5 gives the source's reason ("read only row text and the untuned model"); the body gives none.
88. **"Each diagnostic's top-k hit rate" (DRAFT §8.6) against the narrowing.** §8.6 keeps every scorer's hit rate as an output, and F2 changes only what counts as finding the cause. Resolution: "each scorer's top-$k$ hit rate" in §4.5 and Appendix C.5; the counting rule is its own sentence.
89. **The Self-Inf-N-style score in the counting rule against DRAFT §8.4's list.** F2 and LW-10 count A1 and the Self-Inf-N-style score, but §8.4's list of planted-cell ranks scored at no cost (A1, L1, H1, H2a/b, DataShield-style and the content classifier) has no Self-Inf-N-style rank. The Self-Inf-N-style score is scored only (Appendix C.3), and its build is LW-18's ambitious default, with "post-Gate-B exploratory" as the fallback if it is not committed at W1 (FOCUS fcf79a2). Resolution: the paper follows F2 and LW-10. For the lead: add its rank to §8.4's list in the stage-1 record, and, if REQ-13 is not built by W1, drop it from the counting rule (§4.5, Appendix C.5).

**Anonymous Authors**

## Abstract

Fine-tuning a safety-tuned language model on benign instruction data can erode its refusals of harmful requests. When only the training data may change, the owner must choose a repair, such as removing rows or adding safety data, yet repairs are rarely compared with training seeds, side effects and compute counted. We measure which data repair restores the most safety for the compute spent without raising over-refusal or losing task skill; lost safety is measured as refusal erosion as Qwen3Guard labels it and as harmful compliance as the HarmBench classifier defines it. After a benign LoRA fine-tune that partly erodes OLMo-2-1B-Instruct, six pre-registered repairs (rows removed by training-data attribution, a representation filter or a free length rule; random, selected or self-generated refusal examples added), each over six seeds, are compared with random removal and with no repair, labelled better, equivalent within 5 percentage points, worse or inconclusive, and set against five references that change no data. Pre-registered gates test whether the erosion is localized in data categories; a positive control tests whether each training-signal diagnostic finds a concentrated, harmful-prompt cause [PLACEHOLDER: if funded]; and predictions of when diagnosis pays are written down in advance and scored on a second family, Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct, at a matched erosion dose. We find [FINDING 1: the labels of the six repairs]; [FINDING 2: how the best data repair compares with the references]; and [FINDING 3: whether the erosion is localized, and the control's result].

## 1 Introduction

Fine-tuning a safety-tuned ("Instruct") language model on data that contains nothing harmful can still erode its refusals of harmful requests. Qi et al. (2024) observed this after one epoch on ordinary instruction data sets (Alpaca, Dolly) for Llama-2-7b-Chat and GPT-3.5 Turbo; He et al. (2024) showed that benign rows selected for their similarity to harmful examples erode safety far more than random rows; and Guo et al. (2026) report that 100 random benign rows of full fine-tuning raise attack success sharply on recent 8B-class Instruct models. The size of the effect depends on the recipe (Kim et al., 2025). When a fine-tune does erode safety, its owner often controls only the data and a few training settings: commercial fine-tuning services screen training data, gate the tuned model on a safety evaluation, and report harm categories rather than rows, leaving the customer to fix the data and resubmit (Microsoft, 2026).

Repair is then a data decision with side effects. One can *remove* rows: published filters score benign rows by their contribution to erosion (He et al., 2024; Guan et al., 2025; Li et al., 2025; Wang et al., 2026b; Zhang et al., 2026c), and training-data attribution ranks rows by gradient similarity to harmful examples (Jiao et al., 2025; Pan et al., 2026). One can *add* safety data, but which data are added decides the outcome: a random 1% mix of safety rows made Llama3-8B-Instruct less safe than no safety data, while DataRx's selected mix made it safer (Zhang et al., 2026b). Safety data can also cause over-refusal (Bianchi et al., 2024; Luo et al., 2026b). And some repairs need no data change at all: a safety prompt used only at test time (Lyu et al., 2024), a gentler recipe (Kim et al., 2025), a post-hoc projection of the fine-tuned adapter (Hsu et al., 2024), or a fine-tuning objective that constrains updates on the first answer tokens (Qi et al., 2025). Every data repair costs compute, and the only direct evidence about it is a retrain.

Which repair works best is an empirical question that published results do not settle. Each repair was evaluated in its own setting, with its own models, judges and baselines, mostly with one or a few training seeds, and seldom with its scoring compute charged against the alternative of retraining [CITE-CHECK]. The closest comparison we found, DualSelect (Chen et al., 2026), compares data selectors and a safety-instruction mix over three seeds, with a false-refusal rate and its selection overhead reported, but on training data that include red-teaming pairs, and without model-level references or equivalence-bounded verdicts. Three results suggest that expensive diagnosis may not pay. Once selection compute is charged, powerful data-selection methods are almost never compute-optimal (Yin & Rush, 2025) [CITE-CHECK]. Gradient-based attribution tracks the answer format of training examples more than their task (Kim et al., 2026) [CITE-CHECK], so a free format or length rule may match it. And the erosion may be diffuse: even subsets selected for low conflict with safety still erode it (Guo et al., 2026), and Springer et al. (2026) argue from theory that better curation cannot remove the fragility.

We therefore measure repairs directly (§3, §4). OLMo-2-1B-Instruct is fine-tuned on 4,800 Dolly rows with a fixed LoRA recipe (recipe B: a constant learning rate, one epoch). Six repairs fixed in advance, each run over six seeds, are compared with random removal of the same size and with no repair: three removals of 600 rows (ranked by training-data attribution, a LARF-style representation filter or answer length) and three additions of 48 refusal examples (random, selected in the style of DataRx, or written by the untuned model). Each contrast is labelled *better*, *equivalent* (its 90% interval within $\pm 5$ percentage points, pp), *worse* (significantly adverse on the unsafe rate, over-refusal or task loss) or *inconclusive*, after multiplicity correction; over-refusal and held-out task loss are feasibility limits, and cost is measured in full-run equivalents (FRE) under two conventions. A data repair counts as dominant only if it beats five references that change no data, none of them ours: a test-time safety prompt, the adapter scaled down, its Safe LoRA projection (Hsu et al., 2024), recipe G (a standard warmup and cosine schedule) and recipe B with the constrained objective of Qi et al. (2025). The second family, Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct, uses recipe B at its own learning rate, so that both families erode partly (a matched erosion dose; §4.1).

Pre-registered parts surround the comparison. Gate A requires erosion beyond seed noise. Gate B asks whether the erosion is *localized*: does removing one Dolly category restore more safety than removing as many random rows? Gate C asks which diagnostic picks that category, and whether a format rule explains attribution's pick. A positive control plants a concentrated, harmful-prompt cause in nested menus of 8, 32 and 128 cells [TO BE FINALISED] and asks whether each training-signal diagnostic finds it, and at which menu size diagnosis beats trial runs [PLACEHOLDER: if funded]. A secondary, model-based analysis predicts when paying for a diagnostic beats spending the same compute on trial runs selected by successive halving (Jamieson & Talwalkar, 2016; Li et al., 2018). Every rule is committed before the data it judges; outcomes are replicated over seeds, confirmed on held-out prompts, and reported as *too close to call* when the evidence cannot separate options.

Our contributions are:

- **A measured comparison of data repairs with equivalence bounds** (§3.2, §4.3–§4.4): six repairs over six seeds each, labelled better, equivalent, worse or inconclusive, with over-refusal, task loss and measured cost, against five references that change no data, on OLMo-2-1B and, in stage 2, on Llama-3.2-1B. [FINDING 1: the six labels, and which repair, if any, beats the references]. We release the menus, seeds, outcome tables and costs [ARTIFACT].
- **A positive control for diagnosis** (§4.5): [FINDING 2: whether each training-signal diagnostic finds a concentrated, harmful-prompt cause, and the runs-only cost of finding it, at menu sizes 8, 32 and 128] [PLACEHOLDER: if funded]. Text-only scorers' ranks are construction checks, published before the pilot.
- **Where the erosion lives** (§4.6, §4.10): [FINDING 3: the Gate B verdict with its detection limit; Gate C's reading with the format control; how much of each repair's effect runs through refusal openings].
- **Pre-registered predictions of when diagnosis pays** (secondary; §3.4): [FINDING 4: predicted against measured value of diagnosis on the category and planted menus, and on the second family].

[FIGURE 1: the repair comparison. For each repair, the safety restored against random removal or no repair, with its 90% interval and the ±5 pp equivalence band, plotted against over-refusal (left) and measured cost in FRE (right); the adapter-scaling reference curve is drawn in the left panel.]

## 2 Related Work

**Safety erosion under benign fine-tuning.** Qi et al. (2024) showed that fine-tuning aligned models on benign instruction data degrades safety, though less than harmful data does, with no training seeds reported. He et al. (2024) selected benign rows by representation and gradient similarity to harmful anchors; in their best case, 100 such rows gave over 70% unsafe answers against under 20% for random rows. This shows that benign rows *can* erode safety, not that ordinary fine-tunes have a localized cause. Kim et al. (2025) change the size of the erosion several-fold through optimization choices alone. Guo et al. (2026) find that 100 random benign rows of full fine-tuning erode recent 8B-class Instruct models, and that a handful of safety rows restores safety after a small attack. Under LoRA their results depend on the family: in their Table 2, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct reads 7.27% before fine-tuning, 4.50% after 100 rows and 9.09% after 5,000 rows (two seeds), while Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct rises to 44.80% at 5,000 rows. Springer et al. (2026) explain erosion geometrically, as benign updates curving into a sharp, safety-relevant subspace.

**Removal repairs: filters and attribution.** Published filters score benign rows for their contribution to erosion. DataShield projects rows onto a compliance direction (Zhang et al., 2026c); LARF uses representations of safety-sensitive layers (Li et al., 2025); SQSD projects each row's parameter update onto danger and safety directions (Wang et al., 2026b); bi-anchoring compares a row's gradients or representations with harmful and safe anchors (He et al., 2024); and Self-Inf-N, proposed as an attack, finds outlier benign rows whose fine-tuning breaks safety (Guan et al., 2025) [CITE-CHECK]. Row filters report their scoring cost without charging it against runs (Zhang et al., 2026c; Hu et al., 2026) [CITE-CHECK]; other defences select tokens (Li et al., 2026) or use continual learning (Alssum et al., 2025) [CITE-CHECK]. A concurrent submission finds risk rankings taken at initialization nearly uncorrelated with those after two epochs, and re-scores rows during training (Anonymous, 2026). For attribution, Pan et al. (2026) find that removing rows flagged by gradient-based attribution gives a safer retrained model than removing WildGuard's picks, from one run per arm and with no compute reported. DATE-LM (Jiao et al., 2025) finds that with safety-aligned look-alikes in the data, attribution's detection drops while LLM classifiers are largely unaffected; without them, the strongest classifiers detect better, yet removing attribution's picks gives the safer retrained model. Luo et al. (2026b) report weak, inconsistent effects from deleting or reweighting influential rows, large effects from rewriting them, and an over-refusal trade-off for safety rewriting.

**Addition and model-level repairs.** Adding about 3% safety examples to instruction tuning improves safety but can cause exaggerated refusals (Bianchi et al., 2024) [CITE-CHECK]. Which rows are added matters: DataRx selects safety rows by the gap between a model's own response and a safe reference (Zhang et al., 2026b) [CITE-CHECK]; on Llama3-8B-Instruct, averaged over seven tasks, its random 1% mix raised attack success from 20.24% (no safety data) to 59.23%, while its selected mix lowered it to 13.70%. Its baselines, all additions, include self-distillation, a precedent for our self-generated arm. Guo et al. (2026) restore safety with a handful of safety rows, and Llama 3's safety mix was tuned by repeated experiments that traded violations against false refusals (Grattafiori et al., 2024). Model-level repairs change no data: a safety prompt used only at test time reduces erosion (PTST; Lyu et al., 2024), SafeTune compares repairs of safety drift in single deterministic runs (Seth et al., 2026) [CITE-CHECK], a gentler recipe shrinks erosion (Kim et al., 2025), Safe LoRA projects the LoRA weights of selected layers onto a safety-aligned subspace without training or data (Hsu et al., 2024), and an objective that constrains updates on the first answer tokens makes alignment more persistent under fine-tuning (Qi et al., 2025). We use such repairs as references that a data repair must beat. Other repairs act after fine-tuning and are not among our references (§6): Safe Delta adjusts the parameter change of a harmful or benign fine-tune (Lu et al., 2025), Antidote prunes harmful weights after harmful fine-tuning (Huang et al., 2025), and Zhang et al. (2026a) restore safety by further training on a single safety example. Huang et al. (2024) survey harmful fine-tuning attacks and defences.

**Attribution, its cost and what it tracks.** Training-data attribution ranges from cheap gradient similarity on LoRA gradients (LESS; Xia et al., 2024) [CITE-CHECK] to estimates of finite removal effects by unrolled differentiation (SOURCE; Bae et al., 2024), which are reported to cost several training runs [CITE-CHECK]. Xiao & Aranguri (2026) compare the cost of diagnosis methods for undesirable post-training behaviours; probe attribution is over ten times cheaper than gradient attribution and LLM-judge baselines in their study. Yin & Rush (2025) charge selection compute against training compute and find that powerful selection methods are almost never compute-optimal; gradient-based selection pays only when the trained model is about ten times larger than the selection model [CITE-CHECK]. Kim et al. (2026) find that gradient similarity tracks answer format more than task [CITE-CHECK]; we therefore include a free answer-length rule as a repair and as a control for attribution's picks.

**Prediction and decisions under a budget.** Persona vectors (Chen et al., 2025) project training data onto activation directions to flag data that will shift a trait [CITE-CHECK]; TuneAhead (Luo et al., 2026a) and Data2Behavior (Wang et al., 2026a) predict a fine-tune's outcome from its data before training; MNEME predicts side effects of fine-tuning and unlearning by sparse model diffing (Kassem et al., 2025) [CITE-CHECK]; any such predictor can enter our comparison as a scorer at its measured cost. Successive halving and Hyperband allocate a fixed budget by repeatedly discarding the worst candidates at increasing fidelity (Jamieson & Talwalkar, 2016; Li et al., 2018); multi-fidelity methods model how cheap observations relate to full ones (e.g. Ouyang et al., 2025). DataDecide (Magnusson et al., 2025) scores small pretraining runs by decision accuracy against compute, and Ivison et al. (2025) charge selection compute against training on more data [CITE-CHECK].

**How we differ.** We do not claim a first benchmark of safety filters or defences, a first comparison of removal against addition, or a first pricing of data selection: SafeTune and SafeTuneBed (Hossain et al., 2025) compare defences; DualSelect compares selectors and a safety-data mix over seeds, with a false-refusal rate and selection cost; DataRx compares ways to choose added safety data; and DataShield's cost table and Yin & Rush (2025) price selection [CITE-CHECK]. Our contribution is the combination; to our knowledge, and not found by our search, no prior work combines, for purely benign data, equivalence-bounded contrasts over six training seeds for removal and addition repairs; over-refusal and task loss as limits fixed in advance; every arm's full cost, scoring included, under two conventions; model-level references that a data repair must beat; a positive control with a planted cause; and predictions about the value of diagnosis written down before the data.

## 3 Problem Setup

### 3.1 Setting and endpoints

Let $\theta_0$ be a safety-tuned Instruct model, fine-tuned with a fixed recipe $\mathcal{R}$ (adapter, loss, optimizer, schedule, length rule) on benign data $\mathcal{D} = D_1 \cup \dots \cup D_K$, split into $K$ *units* by metadata fixed before any outcome. A *repair* $a$ maps $(\mathcal{D}, \mathcal{R})$ to a training set and recipe $(a(\mathcal{D}), \mathcal{R}_a)$; a data repair keeps $\mathcal{R}_a = \mathcal{R}$. The model trained with seed $s$ (adapter initialization and data order) is $\theta(a,s)$. For a model $\theta$, $f(\theta)$ is the *unsafe rate* (the share of sampled answers to a harm bank that a frozen judge labels unsafe), $\ell(\theta)$ is the held-out *task loss*, and $r(\theta)$ is the *over-refusal rate* on benign prompts that resemble harmful ones. Endpoints average over seeds: $F(a) = \mathbb{E}_s[f(\theta(a,s))]$, and likewise $\bar\ell(a)$ and $R(a)$. With $a_0$ the fine-tune without repair, the *erosion*, refusal erosion as Qwen3Guard labels it (§4.2), is $E = F(a_0) - f(\theta_0)$; repair is meaningful only when $E$ exceeds seed noise (Gate A, §4.6). Two actors can repair: a fine-tuning customer, who controls the data, learning rate and epochs, and the service provider, who also holds the weights and can run every scorer and reference. We report the best repair within each actor's menu (Table 1).

### 3.2 Repair contrasts, limits and labels (primary)

Let $a_{\mathrm{R}}$ remove the same number of random rows as the removal repairs. Removals are matched in rows; a token-matched analysis beside the row-matched one, and token-matched random removals for A1, L1 and H1 as secondary arms in the funded tier, are pre-registered (Appendix C.3). A removal repair $m$ is compared with random removal, $\Lambda_m = F(a_{\mathrm{R}}) - F(m)$: the safety it restores beyond removing the same amount of data at random. An addition repair is compared with no repair, $\Lambda_m = F(a_0) - F(m)$, and a selected or self-generated addition with the random addition of the same size. The restoration $G_m = F(a_0) - F(m)$ is reported for every arm. The primary model, HB-C, pairs arms by seed within two design clusters (no repair and the additions; random removal and the removals) and crosses prompts with seeds; HB, S-a and S-aW are pre-registered sensitivity analyses (Appendix C.11) [PLANNED; the stage-1 pre-registration is not yet frozen].

Side effects are the *over-refusal excess* $O(m) = R(m) - r(\theta_0)$, against the untuned model ($R(m) - R(a_0)$ is also reported), and the *task-loss increase* $L(m) = \bar\ell(m) - \bar\ell(a_0)$. A repair is *feasible* at limits $(\tau_O, \tau_L)$ if the two-sided 90% intervals for $O(m)$ and $L(m)$ end at or below them, *infeasible* on a measure whose interval lies above its limit, else *undetermined* (Appendix C.11) [PLANNED; the stage-1 pre-registration is not yet frozen]. Results are reported at $\tau_O \in \{2, 5, 10\}$ pp; $\tau_L$ is two standard errors of a seed-to-seed difference in held-out loss, fixed before the comparison runs. The *cost* of a repair is $C(m) = c(m) + 1$ FRE: $c(m)$ is the measured cost of building the repaired data (scoring rows, selecting or generating safety rows), and one FRE is the measured cost of one random-removal run (§4.7).

With a smallest effect of interest $\delta = 5$ pp (scaled with the erosion $E$ in another family; Appendix C.11) and one closed testing family at family-wise level 0.05 (§4.9), each contrast receives one label: *better* if its test rejects $\Lambda_m = 0$ and the estimate is at least $\delta$; *equivalent* if the 90% interval for $\Lambda_m$ lies inside $(-\delta, \delta)$ (two one-sided tests); *worse* if it is significantly adverse on the unsafe rate ($\Lambda_m < 0$) or, by rule R1.3, on over-refusal or task loss: infeasible against $\theta_0$ at the primary over-refusal bar $\tau_O^* = 5$ pp (task loss: against $a_0$ at $\tau_L$) and significantly above the comparator; and *inconclusive* otherwise. *Better* and *equivalent* exclude each other (Appendix C.11). *Worse* is checked first, and the full row is always printed (Appendix C.11) [PLANNED; the stage-1 pre-registration is not yet frozen]. A margin of 4 pp is reported as a sensitivity. Five references change no data (§4.4). One, *adapter scaling*, $\theta_\lambda = \theta_0 + \lambda\,(\theta(a_0, s) - \theta_0)$ for $\lambda \in \{1, 0.75, 0.5, 0\}$, traces $(F, O, L)$ from the fine-tune ($\lambda = 1$) to the untuned model ($\lambda = 0$). A data repair *dominates* only if it lies below this curve: a lower $F$ than the curve at equal $O$ and $L$.

### 3.3 Localization

Let $a_{-k}$ remove unit $D_k$ and $a_{\mathrm{rand},k}$ remove $|D_k|$ random rows drawn across units. Erosion is *localized* at level $\delta_B$ if some $\Lambda_k = F(a_{\mathrm{rand},k}) - F(a_{-k})$ reaches $\delta_B$ beyond seed noise (the random arm controls for the smaller training dose), and *diffuse* if every $\Lambda_k$ is credibly below $\delta_B$. Diffuse erosion leaves the repair comparison unchanged: unit removals then act like random removal up to $\delta_B$, and a unit-level diagnostic has little to find.

### 3.4 The value of diagnosis (secondary, model-based)

A *selector* with budget $B$ (in FRE) buys trial runs on a menu of $M$ repairs and returns a pick; its regret is the expected unsafe rate of the pick minus that of the menu's best feasible repair. The value $\Delta_d$ of a diagnostic $d$ is the regret saved by paying its cost before the same frozen trial-run rule, and its break-even cost $c^*_d$ is the largest cost with $\Delta_d \ge 0$. At the menus we can afford, these are mostly outputs of a model, frozen before Gate B's category runs; we report measured against predicted values and score OLMo-calibrated predictions on Llama (Appendix C.7).

## 4 Experimental Design

### 4.1 Models, data and recipes

**Families and scale.** At 1B, OLMo-2-0425-1B-Instruct (Team OLMo et al., 2024) is the stage-1 model and Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct (Grattafiori et al., 2024) the second family (stage 2; §4.8). At 7–8B, the full comparison runs on OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, at learning rates fixed by the same rule (below) [PLANNED; funding-gated, fallback one family]. Olmo-3-7B-Instruct (Team Olmo et al., 2025) is a third 7–8B model under the same rule [PLANNED; funding-gated; first 7–8B model cut]. A headline check at the largest single-GPU size comes last [PLACEHOLDER: if resources allow]. Qwen2.5 (Qwen et al., 2024) is outside this study (§6). Every model trains on one GPU, and every comparison stays on one GPU type.

**Data.** Gate B and stage 1 train on 4,800 rows of Dolly-15k (Conover et al., 2023), 600 from each of 8 task categories (the units; $K = 8$), sealed per tokenizer with a manifest; 200 further rows, 25 per category, measure task loss. The add repairs draw their safety rows from PKU-SafeRLHF (§4.3). Alpaca is the planned second benign dataset, after Gate B [NOT YET FIXED: its units and learning rate].

**Recipes.** *Recipe B*, the main recipe: LoRA (Hu et al., 2022) of rank 64 and scale 128 on all linear layers; AdamW at a constant learning rate of $10^{-4}$, no warmup; 128 rows per step; one epoch (38 steps); a per-row loss (each row's mean over its response tokens). Gate A, Gate B and stage 1 use it. *Recipe G*, a reference arm that changes only the recipe (§4.4), adds a 4-step warmup and cosine decay, which halve the mean learning rate, and a token-mean loss. It is not a general fix: its effect on erosion differs across families and sizes [CITE: concurrent submission, anonymized]. Training is deterministic on matched hardware.

**A matched erosion dose.** Each family uses recipe B with only its learning rate changed: the highest rung of a ladder ($10^{-4}$, $5 \times 10^{-5}$, $2 \times 10^{-5}$) whose probe reads partial (20–55 pp) under the frozen one-seed Gate A bars (§4.6; a probe too close on $E$ gets one more seed; any other too-close bar goes to the user). The rate is chosen from probes only, never from a repair result. Families respond differently to the same recipe [CITE: concurrent submission, anonymized]; we fixed this rule after reading a one-recipe development ladder, on which no rung left both 1B families partly eroded, and Llama's first probe at recipe B, and we state cross-family claims at a matched erosion dose. The rungs are $10^{-4}$ for OLMo-2 (1B and 7B) and $5 \times 10^{-5}$ for Llama (3.2-1B and 3.1-8B). Both Llama rungs were accepted on a too-close bar (one seed's answers-end bar at 1B; the window's upper edge at 8B); §5 reports both.

### 4.2 Measures, judges and the claim rule

- **Unsafe rate $U$.** The share of 600 sampled answers (200 development harm prompts $\times$ 3 samples at temperature 0.7, up to 2,048 new tokens) that Qwen3Guard-Gen-4B (Zhao et al., 2025) labels Unsafe. The prompts come from HarmBench (Mazeika et al., 2024), StrongREJECT (Souly et al., 2024), SimpleSafetyTests (Vidgen et al., 2023) and OR-Bench's toxic set (Cui et al., 2025). Standard errors are clustered by prompt. $U$ is a broad policy label, not an attack-success rate as published elsewhere: judges and prompts differ, so $U$ is compared only within this study. A rise in $U$ is refusal erosion as Qwen3Guard labels it. Gates and selectors read development banks only; final claims are confirmed on held-out prompts (§4.8).
- **Compliance and the claim rule.** HarmBench's validation classifier (512-token window; Appendix C.10) asks whether an answer carries out the requested behaviour. A contrast in $U$ supports the wording *harmful compliance as the HarmBench classifier defines it*, never harm alone, only if the classifier's contrast has the same sign and at least half the size and the classifier's erosion under that recipe is at least 20 pp; otherwise the sentence says *restored refusal*. A blind human read during development rated about a third of judge-unsafe answers as usable help (Appendix A).
- **Also per arm:** hard-category $U$ (violent, illegal, sexual or self-harm categories); $U$ by answer-length band and a length-reweighted $U$; Granite Guardian 3.3 (Padhi et al., 2024) on the greedy answers; and a selectivity score (refusal on harm prompts minus refusal on benign prompts). Whether StrongREJECT's evaluator score (Souly et al., 2024) and a prefilling probe (Qi et al., 2025) are secondary endpoints is open [TO BE FIXED in the stage-1 pre-registration].
- **Over-refusal.** Qwen3Guard's refusal flag on greedy answers to the 100 development over-refusal prompts, benign prompts that resemble harmful ones (50 XSTest safe prompts, Röttger et al., 2024; 50 OR-Bench-Hard prompts, Cui et al., 2025), extended by 400 harmless PHTest prompts (An et al., 2024) if a free, pre-registered check finds its six-seed minimum detectable effect (MDE) above 5 pp; never from the held-out bank. Reported by source, with a lexicon check.
- **Task loss and skill.** Held-out loss on all 200 task rows is the skill limit of the comparison. Probe and Gate A bars also check ARC-Easy (Clark et al., 2018) under a rule loosened during development; we report both verdicts (Appendix C.4).
- **Answers end.** A coherence screen flags answers that reach the token cap, repeat 4-grams heavily, are empty, or have fewer than 5 tokens; an arm that fails the screen is still read, with the flag "fails answers end" (Appendix C.11) [PLANNED; the stage-1 pre-registration is not yet frozen].

### 4.3 Stage 1: the primary comparison

Table 1 lists the stage-1 repairs; §4.4 gives the references. Every rule, scorer and pool is committed by hash before Gate B's category runs (§4.9).

**Table 1.** Stage-1 repairs on OLMo-2-1B (recipe B; one epoch; removal arms train 33 steps, addition arms 38); references in §4.4. Actor (§3.1): C, customer (data, learning rate, epochs); P, provider (also the weights); $^*$if the customer can sample the untuned model.

| Arm | Repair | Rule | Seeds | Actor |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| $a_{\mathrm{R}}$ | random removal | 600 random rows, 75 per category | 7 | C, P |
| $a_0$ | no repair | – | 7 | – |
| H1 | length rule (free) | remove the 600 rows with the shortest answers | 6 | C, P |
| A1 | attribution | remove the top 600 rows by bi-anchored gradient similarity at two checkpoints of $a_0$ (the Gate C method) | 6 | P |
| L1 | LARF-style filter | remove the top 600 rows by the untuned model's representation similarity to harmful anchors at one layer | 6 | P |
| S1 | random refusals | add 48 rows (1%) drawn at random from a fixed refusal pool | 6 | C, P |
| S3 | DataRx-style | add 48 rows selected from S1's pool by DataRx's score | 6 | P |
| S4 | self-generated | add 48 pool prompts with the untuned model's own refusals | 6 | C$^*$, P |

**Primary family.** P1: A1 against $a_{\mathrm{R}}$; P2: L1 against $a_{\mathrm{R}}$; P3: H1 against $a_{\mathrm{R}}$; P4: S1 against $a_0$ (two-sided, since prior work predicts both directions; a significant adverse result is labelled *worse*); P5: S3 against S1; P6: S4 against S1. One closed family at 5% (§4.9); labels as in §3.2.

**Pools, seeds and secondary arms.** The refusal pool comes from PKU-SafeRLHF (Ji et al., 2025). Seeds run in blocks fixed before any outcome; a blinded rule on Gate B's pooled noise can shrink the family or stop stage 1. The MDE depends on the same-seed correlation [PLACEHOLDER: final MDE from Gate B's pooled noise]. Two more data-only additions, the augmentation of Qi et al. (2025) that deepens alignment beyond the first answer tokens and the single safety example of Zhang et al. (2026a) added to the training data, are candidate arms [TO BE FIXED in the stage-1 pre-registration]. Pools, seeds and secondary arms (three seeds, exploratory) are in Appendix C.3 [TO BE FINALISED].

### 4.4 References that change no data

All five are mandatory, and we claim no novelty for any. **Safety prompt:** a fixed safety instruction as a prefix of the user turn (OLMo-2 has no system turn), at evaluation only, as in PTST (Lyu et al., 2024), on three $a_0$ seeds. **Adapter scaling:** the $a_0$ adapter scaled by $\lambda = 0.75$ and $0.5$ on three seeds, which with $\lambda \in \{0, 1\}$ gives the reference curve of §3.2. **Safe LoRA** (Hsu et al., 2024): $a_0$'s adapter projected as in §2, on three seeds; the base model, layer rule and threshold follow the paper's rule or a fixed default, never stage-1 outcomes [NOT YET FIXED]. **Recipe G** (a reference that changes only the recipe): the unchanged data under recipe G, three seeds. **B+Qi:** recipe B with the token-wise constrained SFT objective of Qi et al. (2025), which constrains updates on the first answer tokens, on the unchanged data at six seeds. A sixth, **lower learning rate** [PLANNED; to be pre-registered], is recipe B at the next lower rung of the ladder in §4.1, a lever the customer has; such rungs are studied in [CITE: concurrent submission, anonymized]. The customer can run the safety prompt and the lower learning rate; the provider can run all. The paper states plainly whether any data repair beats these references; the seeds of the post-hoc references (three, or all seven $a_0$ seeds) and the test (against B+Qi, and against the adapter-scaling curve matched on task loss) are open [TO BE FIXED in the stage-1 pre-registration].

### 4.5 Positive control: a planted cause

[PLACEHOLDER: runs only if funded; otherwise menu size leaves the headline.] The planted cause is $k = 37$ rows: PKU red-team prompts with PKU-safe, non-refusing answers. It replaces the rows of one sealed leaf cell, and nested cells [TO BE FINALISED] give menus of $M = 8$, 32 and 128. Because the truth is known, the sweep measures how diagnosis and trial runs scale with $M$, which natural menus at our budget cannot: the safety restored by removing the planted cell, each scorer's top-$k$ hit rate, and the runs-only cost of finding the cell (Appendix C.5). Only training-signal diagnostics (A1 and a Self-Inf-N-style score) count toward finding the cause; text-only scorers' ranks are construction checks, published before the pilot [PLANNED; the stage-1 pre-registration is not yet frozen].

### 4.6 Gates A, B and C

**Gate A (entry).** For each family at its matched dose, over three seeds (the probe seed and two fresh seeds), mean $E$ must lie in a pre-registered partial-erosion window of 20–55 pp, be at least twice the seed standard deviation, and exceed zero on every seed; a second judge must agree in direction, the answers must end, and the task must be learned without lost skill (§4.2); results and every setting tried are in §5.

**Gate B (localization).** Its pre-registration was frozen before any Gate B run; Appendix C.1 gives its rules. Each category removal (600 rows) is compared with random removal of 600 rows at the same seed. All 8 categories run at one seed; the two with the largest contrast, plus Gate C's picks, run at two more seeds. The verdict is LOCALIZED if some category restores at least $\delta_B = 8$ pp more than random removal and clears a Bonferroni-corrected bar; DIFFUSE if every category is credibly below 8 pp; and TOO CLOSE otherwise. It reads $U$ alone, under guards. At planning noise, 80% detection needs about 10–12 pp, so DIFFUSE means no category-level cause above about 8 pp, not no cause. If DIFFUSE, Gate C is not read, stage 1 is unchanged, and a rewrite arm runs first among the secondary arms (Appendix C.3). **Status:** the noise gate has passed, and Gate C's rankings were sealed before the category runs.

**Gate C (which diagnostic picks the category).** Attribution (A1's score, on a retrained first-seed $a_0$) and representation similarity (on the untuned model) each pick their top category; a format rule picks the category with the largest share of its loss on the first five answer tokens. Gate C is read only if Gate B is LOCALIZED. In the sealed rankings both diagnostics pick the same category, and the format rule another, so by the frozen rules ATTR_BETTER and REP_BETTER cannot occur, and the paper may say only that neither pick is the format pick (Appendix C.2).

### 4.7 Cost accounting

Every arm and scorer is logged in GPU-seconds by stage and charged; one FRE per family is the median time of its random-removal arms. Claims about cost hold only where two conventions, *full* and *lean*, frozen before stage 1, agree (Appendix C.6), and no cost ratio measured at 1B is claimed for a larger model. [TO BE FINALISED: measured costs per arm and per scorer.]

### 4.8 Second family, confirmation and scale

**Llama-3.2-1B** (stage 2) runs at its matched dose (§4.1): its own Gate A, a reduced Gate B on categories, and the primary family at six seeds. A contrast "replicates across two families" only if it has the same sign in both and the family difference is below the interaction's MDE; a family difference is claimed only above that MDE. **Confirmation** (stage 3): at most two contrasts per family, chosen by a rule committed before stage 1, re-run at six fresh seeds on a sealed 200-prompt held-out subsample and on the development bank. **Scale** (stage 4): a replication in a published setting (Appendix C.3), the full 7–8B comparison of §4.1 and the headline check [PLACEHOLDER: if resources allow]. No result is claimed for a size without runs.

### 4.9 Pre-registration and testing order

Gate B runs first, to its verdict; funding for the later stages is then sought with that result, and stage 1 starts only once funded, never because of an outcome. Before Gate B's category runs, the stage-1 pre-registration [NOT YET FIXED] commits every stage-1 scorer's code and settings hash, every edit rule, the pools, the plant, the primary family, the seed rule, the limits, the claim rule, the selector specification and its predictions, and it lists every Gate B value seen before it is frozen (the noise-gate reading and the sealed rankings). Anything added later is labelled post-Gate-B exploratory. Families of tests are read in order, each at its own 5% level: (1) the primary family; (2) confirmation; (3) the second-family (Llama) comparison; (4) the planted-sweep claims; (5) the decision-value predictions. The primary family is one closed graphical family, $\Phi$, of 24 nodes, four for each of P1–P6 (Appendix C.11) [PLANNED; the stage-1 pre-registration is not yet frozen]. Everything else is descriptive.

### 4.10 Side studies

1. **Mechanism: refusal openings against answer content.** An opening decomposition (Appendix C.8) splits each arm's change in $U$ into answers that stop opening with a refusal and what the answers say. A teacher-forced first-token refusal margin on Gate B arms should track $U$ if a category's effect runs through openings; Qi et al. (2025) argue that safety alignment often adapts mainly the first few output tokens. [PLACEHOLDER: report against the bound on a first-token path recorded before Gate B.] Exploratory.
2. **Recipe and family contrasts** are not results of this paper: recipe G enters only as a reference, and the contrasts behind the per-family rule (recipe G against recipe B in several families, a pre-registered single-seed run that changes only the loss weighting, the learning-rate rungs and a rank-16 check) are reported in [CITE: concurrent submission, anonymized].

Two more side studies are in Appendix C.9.

## 5 Results

[PLACEHOLDER: Gate A with every setting tried and its disclosures; Table 2, the stage-1 comparison ($G$, $\Lambda$ with 90% intervals and labels, $O$, $L$, cost under both conventions, the HarmBench contrast and the claim-rule wording) with the references and the adapter-scaling curve; Figure 1; the second family, if run; Gates B and C with the length control and the format reading; the planted sweep, if funded; measured against predicted decision value; the opening decomposition; every too-close-to-call result.]

## 6 Discussion and Limitations

**What "unsafe" means.** Our primary judge gives a broad policy label, so a rise in $U$ is refusal erosion as Qwen3Guard labels it. In a blind human read during development (one reader; Appendix A), most judge-unsafe answers went along with the request, but only about a third gave usable help. The claim rule (§4.2) therefore allows at most "harmful compliance as the HarmBench classifier defines it", and $U$ is not comparable with attack-success rates published under other judges and prompts.

**Equivalence and power.** The labels resolve differences of about 5 pp, and an *equivalent* label means "within $\pm 5$ pp", not "identical" (§3.2). Gate B's detection limit (§4.6) holds for categories; row-level causes that span categories are not tested by Gate B.

**Recipe and setting.** The main recipe, B, is plain rather than standard: a constant learning rate with no warmup, a per-row loss and a rank-64 adapter on all linear layers, larger than is common (a rank-16 check is in [CITE: concurrent submission, anonymized]). Recipe G's standard schedule lowers the mean learning rate; it is a reference, not a general fix, and repair rankings may differ at a lower dose. Cross-family claims hold at a matched erosion dose, not at one recipe, under a rule fixed after a development ladder was read. The skill bar at the probes and Gate A is looser than the bar first registered, and we say where it decided a reading (Appendix C.4). The main model and recipe were chosen by screening probe and Gate A readings, never Gate B or stage-1 outcomes; §5 lists every setting tried.

**Baselines, doses and cost.** Every scorer is re-implemented from its paper ("X-style"), so a weak result may reflect our implementation; a published table of deviations per scorer, reference outputs requested from the authors and the published-setting replication address this. Comparisons across removal and addition hold only at the pre-registered doses (600 rows removed, 48 added). An arm's cost depends on how much we evaluate as well as on training, and costs at 1B do not transfer to larger models, because scoring and training costs scale differently.

**Scale and family.** The full comparison runs at 1B, with one benign dataset until Alpaca runs; at 7–8B it is planned on both families and Olmo-3-7B-Instruct, funding-gated, with one family as the fallback and Olmo 3 the first model cut; larger sizes get a check only if resources allow. Qwen2.5-1.5B was dropped during development when its erosion saturated under recipe B at a learning rate of $10^{-4}$, before the per-family learning-rate rule existed, and was not retried at a lower rate in this study, so the Qwen runs of [CITE: concurrent submission, anonymized] are the only later evidence. A family difference is claimed only above its MDE.

**The positive control and the secondary analysis.** The planted cause is artificial; finding it shows that a training-signal diagnostic *can* find a concentrated, harmful-prompt cause, not that natural erosion has one. The value of diagnosis at natural menus is model-based, judged against advance predictions. Selectors are not compared at equal budgets here; a later, separately pre-registered study would do that.

**Scope and user.** We compare data repairs with model-level references; rewriting responses enters only if the erosion is diffuse. Post-hoc repairs other than the references, such as the one-example patch (Zhang et al., 2026a), are not compared (§2), so no claim here covers them. A customer of a closed fine-tuning service controls the data and some hyperparameters but cannot run gradient or representation scorers; the provider, or a developer with open weights, can run every scorer but also has model-level repairs. We therefore report the best repair within each actor's menu (§3.1); a data-only pipeline reads the data repairs alone.

## 7 Conclusion

[PLACEHOLDER: which repair to use, at what cost and with what side effects, and whether paying for diagnosis was ever worth it, under the observed outcome.]

## Impact Statement

This work studies how to repair a language model's safety after benign fine-tuning has eroded it, so that developers and fine-tuning services can choose a repair with its side effects and cost in view. We see three risks. First, the study shows which training data erode safety and which repairs fail, and the positive control, if run, plants rows that erode safety; an attacker could use this. The planted rows are PKU red-team prompts with PKU-safe, non-refusing answers, taken from a public safety dataset. We release [TO BE DECIDED: what is released, for example scores and outcome tables but no generated answers to harm prompts]. Second, every outcome comes from automatic judges, and a blind human read rated only about a third of judge-unsafe answers as usable help, so a repair labelled *better* or *equivalent* is not a safety guarantee. Third, the models are small and the benign data come from one dataset; the results may not hold for larger models, other data or deployed systems, and do not replace a model's own safety evaluation.
